# Daily Papers
The project automatically fetches the latest papers from arXiv based on keywords.

The subheadings in the README file represent the search keywords.

Only the most recent articles for each keyword are retained, up to a maximum of 100 papers.

You can click the 'Watch' button to receive daily email notifications.

Last update: 2026-10-20

## RAG

| **[Enhancing RAG-based Medical VQA with Gated Multimodal Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23588v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Medical Vision-Language Models (Med-LVLMs) have shown strong performance in Medical Visual Question Answering (MVQA), yet they may still produce factually inconsistent responses. Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) can mitigate this issue by incorporating external clinical evidence; however, retrieval is often guided by a single modality, potentially overlooking the joint semantics of medical images and clinical questions. We introduce a multimodal RAG framework that learns a joint query representation through a Gated Multimodal Unit, enabling evidence retrieval to leverage information from both modalities while keeping the underlying Med-LVLM frozen. We evaluate the proposed approach on benchmark datasets. Results show that the multimodal query representation consistently outperforms a reference Med-LVLM and remains competitive with state-of-the-art methods. Our findings demonstrate the potential of multimodal query representations for more effective evidence retrieval in Medical VQA.</p></details> | <details><summary>Prepr...</summary><p>Preprint submitted to ECIR</p></details> |
| **[RIT-RAG: Navigating Document Corpora with Retrieval-Induced Trees](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11370v2)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) grounds language models in external corpora. Agentic RAG enables iterative search, yet exposes the model to isolated chunks without document structure, making it difficult to distinguish relevant evidence from chunks that merely resemble the query. Structure-aware methods such as PageIndex navigate using the structure but cannot scale to structures of large corpora that cannot fit in the LLM context. Hence, they first commit to a single document using a document retriever and cannot recover from a wrong choice. We propose RIT-RAG (Retrieval-Induced Tree RAG), which combines content retrieval with structural navigation. Offline, RIT-RAG builds a tree for each document from its table of contents or sitemap. At query time, it retrieves a broad set of chunks and uses their positions to induce manageable sub-trees, potentially across multiple documents. An LLM agent navigate these sub-trees, selectively reads promising nodes, and reformulates queries when needed. Thus, retrieval proposes where to look, while the agent decides what to read. Across financial, scientific, and customer-support benchmarks, RIT-RAG achieves the highest answer accuracy among vanilla, graph-based, and agentic baselines. On EntQABench, our new benchmark of 2.84 million technical-documentation webpages, it improves accuracy by 6.6--11.4 points over the strongest baseline across three LLMs.</p></details> |  |
| **[Operon: Incremental Construction of Ragged Data via Named Dimensions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.16080v2)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Modern data processing workflows frequently encounter ragged data: collections with variable-length elements that arise naturally in domains like natural language processing, scientific measurements, and autonomous AI agents. Existing workflow engines lack native support for tracking the shapes and dependencies inherent to ragged data, forcing users to manage complex indexing and dependency bookkeeping manually. We present Operon, a Rust-based workflow engine that addresses these challenges through a novel formalism of named dimensions with explicit dependency relations. Operon provides a domain-specific language where users declare pipelines with dimension annotations that are statically verified for correctness, while the runtime system dynamically schedules tasks as data shapes are incrementally discovered during execution. We formalize the mathematical foundation for reasoning about partial shapes and prove that Operon's incremental construction algorithm guarantees deterministic and confluent execution in parallel settings. The system's explicit modeling of partially-known states enables robust persistence and recovery mechanisms, while its per-task multi-queue architecture achieves efficient parallelism across heterogeneous task types. Empirical evaluation demonstrates that Operon outperforms an existing workflow engine with 14.94x baseline overhead reduction while maintaining near-linear end-to-end output rates as workloads scale, making it particularly suitable for large-scale data generation pipelines in machine learning applications.</p></details> |  |
| **[FuseRAG: Fusing Structure and Evidence into Inference for Cost-Efficient Graph-based RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22818v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Graph-based retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) exploits relationships among evidence to support multi-hop question answering. However, conveying retrieved graphs through non-unique linear textual contexts to Large Language Models (LLMs) leads to graph-structure degradation and inefficient KV-cache reuse. Meanwhile, graph-structure order optimization is already NP-hard, and the additional KV-cache reuse objective further complicates the search by introducing potentially conflicting orders. To tackle these issues, we propose FuseRAG, a cost-efficient graph-based RAG framework that shifts most retrieved evidence from explicit textual contexts to latent representations. Our main idea is to separate reusable evidence content from query-aware structural interactions in latent space, while retaining only a few explicit facts as semantic anchors. The resulting latent representations are injected into decoder self-attention, where they reshape attention over evidence and thereby influence hidden states and next-token probabilities. This design preserves graph structure through latent interactions, enables evidence reuse beyond exact token-prefix matching. Experiments on three multi-hop question-answering datasets with Llama-3-8B and Qwen3.5-9B show that FuseRAG improves answer quality while achieving up to 2.7x and 2.2x TTFT speedups over the fastest graph-based baseline, respectively.</p></details> |  |
| **[Evidence-Gated Bilingual RAG for Reliable Consumer Banking Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22652v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Consumers often describe what they need rather than use the terminology found on a banking website. For example, a natural-language request ("a card for travel") may not share the keywords of the relevant product page, and the problem is harder in bilingual services, where the same intent may be expressed in Arabic or English. This paper presents an evidence-gated bilingual retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) framework for consumer banking search that operates entirely on local infrastructure. The framework combines structured web-content refinement, hybrid dense-lexical retrieval, intent-aware evidence selection, cross-encoder reranking, and a three-outcome Answer / Related Mismatch / No Answer decision mechanism. On 210 URL-labeled Arabic and English queries, the proposed approach improves Top-1 hit rate from 50.48% to 79.05% and MRR@5 from 0.571 to 0.797 over a hybrid baseline. A separate 26-case end-to-end evaluation achieved 69.2% strict success, with 72.7% of generated answers classified as supported by an automated faithfulness judge. These results are preliminary: the retrieval queries are paraphrases of a small reference set, and the end-to-end test is small and uses an automated rather than human judge, so they do not establish human-verified answer correctness or generalizable performance.</p></details> |  |
| **[When Does Uncertainty Measure RAG Hallucination Risk?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22615v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Uncertainty can measure hallucination risk in retrieval-augmented generation (RAG), but only when retrieved evidence constrains the answer. When evidence is present, unsupported factual claims become less likely; when retrieval coverage is poor, the model can answer from internal knowledge and assign high probability to unsupported claims. We test this across four instruction-tuned models on HotpotQA and SQuAD. After matching faithful and hallucinated answers within word-count bins, length-only classifiers fall near chance, while perplexity reaches 0.81--0.91 AUROC on targeted unsupported edits. Varying retrieval depth shows why the score changes meaning: detection can weaken with only the top-ranked passage, then recover after adding a second passage. A combined uncertainty and semantic-drift score improves calibration, and measurements remain above chance on RAGTruth QA. Results support a conditional view of uncertainty as a useful measure of RAG hallucination risk.</p></details> | <details><summary>6 pag...</summary><p>6 pages. Accepted for oral presentation at ICNLSP 2026</p></details> |
| **[RAG-RST: Retrieved Exemplars as In-Context Discourse Schema for RST Parsing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22150v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In Rhetorical Structure Theory (RST), each edge of a discourse tree carries a nuclearity label, marking which span holds the central content, and a relation drawn from a closed inventory of functional categories. Neither label is a surface property: the same two clauses can be Elaboration or Background depending on rhetorical intent, and the boundary between them is fixed by annotation convention rather than by lexical cues. Predicting these labels therefore requires a formal discourse schema, and exposure to discourse-rich pretraining text does not supply it: given only a target EDU pair, a strong LLM recovers nuclearity at 0.794 F1 and relation at 0.738 F1. We propose RAG-RST, a retrieval-augmented framework that supplies this schema as in-context demonstration rather than through parameter updates, raising nuclearity to 0.846 F1 and relation to 0.788 F1 with no training. Two configurations are compared: a precomputed strategy that labels all EDU pairs before parsing, and a dynamic strategy that retrieves at each reduce step, so that the exemplars reaching the generator depend on the reductions the parser has already committed to. Both outperform existing RST parsers across span, nuclearity, relation, and full tree metrics, with the dynamic variant consistently strongest. Two properties of the retrieved schema emerge: its utility is bounded in number, saturating and then declining beyond fifty exemplars, and it depends on structural position. Both indicate that a formal linguistic schema can be supplied to a foundation model as a demonstration rather than trained into its parameters.</p></details> |  |
| **[Cost-Efficient RAG for Entity Matching with LLMs: A Blocking-based Batch Processing Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.05708v2)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) offer a compelling approach to entity matching (EM) in label-scarce settings, yet their matching quality is sensitive to records with ambiguous or sparse attributes due to the absence of external factual grounding. Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) directly addresses this grounding gap by retrieving and injecting relevant external knowledge. However, naively applying RAG to large-scale EM is expensive due to per-pair retrieval calls over large knowledge graphs (KGs) and the increase in input tokens caused by enriching prompts with retrieved contextual knowledge. To address the above limitation, we introduce a CE-RAG4EM (Cost-Efficient RAG for Entity Matching) framework that reduces overhead through blocking-based batch retrieval and generation. We investigate the benefits of this framework through a systematic analysis of RAG systems for entity matching, focusing on blocking-aware batch optimizations and retrieval granularity. Extensive experiments on nine widely used real-world entity matching benchmark datasets show that CE-RAG4EM under a zero-shot setting achieves comparable or improved matching quality while substantially reducing end-to-end overhead. The results demonstrate that our CE-RAG4EM closes the knowledge grounding gap of the LLM-EM baseline and yields significant improvements in F1 scores on datasets with ambiguous or numerically sparse attributes, while its batch retrieval and inference substantially reduce the overhead and maintain competitive matching quality compared to naive RAG4EM. Our analysis identifies clear performance-overhead trade-offs across blocking-based batch sizes and retrieval granularity, showing how matching quality and computational cost shift with each design choice. These findings offer concrete guidance for building scalable, efficient RAG systems for entity matching that balance accuracy and cost.</p></details> | <details><summary>Exten...</summary><p>Extended version of the paper accepted at ACM SIGMOD 2027</p></details> |
| **[Poisoning the Neighborhood: A Topology-Aware Topic-Level Attack on Large-Scale HNSW-based RAG Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21464v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) mitigates hallucinations and knowledge staleness in large language models using external knowledge. However, ingestion from public and third-party sources exposes RAG systems to poisoning attacks. Existing attacks are designed and evaluated under exact retrieval, where poisoned documents participate in global ranking. Deployment-scale systems instead rely on approximate nearest-neighbor (ANN) indexes such as hierarchical navigable small world (HNSW), where limited search budgets, graph-search reachability, and local benign-document competition invalidate this assumption and degrade existing attacks. We present a topology-aware topic-level poisoning attack against large-scale HNSW-RAG that generalizes to unseen topic queries. We organize poisoned documents into a Hub-Satellite structure: Satellites provide competitive entry points across semantic regions, while Hubs strengthen local cohesion and facilitate cascading retrieval. For generation, we propose an entity-claim promotion attack combining evidence anchoring and entity-claim coupling to promote the target entity via a target claim across different query regions. On 2M-document Wikipedia, our attack maintains over 72% retrieval success and reaches 31.29% attack success, substantially outperforming baselines; domain-specific experiments further support its effectiveness. Structural analyses show that the Hub-Satellite topology forms in HNSW, indicating that graph structure contributes to improved poisoned-document retrieval. Existing poisoning defenses cannot mitigate the attack, exposing graph-structural risks in HNSW-RAG systems.</p></details> |  |
| **[Loud and Clear: Making Visual Evidence Count in Multimodal Document RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21318v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal document retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) has focused largely on retrieval, yet its answers depend on how well a vision-language model reads the retrieved pages. We find that evidence in retrieved pages remains underused even when the correct pages are retrieved. On SciEGQA oracle pages, five prior methods give the generator 0.7K to 2.7K visual tokens per query on average under a common image processor, yet raising the page-only budget from 1K to 32K improves accuracy by 15.22 points. At a fixed 16K budget, adding ground-truth crops improves accuracy by 3.75 points over page-only input. We study what the generator needs from retrieved pages through two requirements: Loud, making full use of visual token capacity, and Clear, refining the page so that its evidence becomes prominent. For Loud, increasing either the visual token budget or visual fidelity, the image resolution those tokens preserve, improves accuracy with the other held fixed, and full-page scaling outperforms repetition and tiling at matched budgets. For Clear, suppressing surrounding content improves accuracy without enlarging selected regions, and adding OCR of these regions to page images outperforms either input alone. We implement both as Loud and Clear (LoC), which combines higher-resolution pages with OCR from query-relevant regions without ground-truth annotations or additional training. LoC outperforms eight prior methods on SciEGQA, ViDoRe-v3, and MMLongBench-Doc, improving over the strongest, ColQwen3, by 8.88, 3.09, and 4.22 points with the same retriever and generator. It also outperforms its page-only variant under the same visual token budget. These results locate a bottleneck beyond retrieval: how the retrieved pages are given to the generator.</p></details> |  |
| **[From One Query to Many: A Critical Survey of Privacy Accounting in RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21245v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) enables large language models to leverage external knowledge but introduces privacy risks when the retrieved corpus contains sensitive information. Differential privacy (DP) offers formal protection, yet interpreting and comparing privacy guarantees across existing DP-RAG methods remains challenging. In particular, a privacy budget reported for a single response does not directly characterize privacy loss over repeated queries, and even identical values of $\varepsilon$ may represent different guarantees depending on the protected data unit, accounting horizon, and observable outputs. This survey critically examines how privacy accounting evolves from single-query generation to multi-query RAG systems. We categorize existing approaches into query-time privatization, multi-query accounting, and one-time corpus privatization. We analyze their underlying assumptions and privacy--utility trade-offs. Rather than ranking methods by $\varepsilon$ alone, we propose a framework that interprets a DP-RAG guarantee along three dimensions: privacy granularity, accounting scope, and release boundaries. We conclude that an $\eps$ is a claim only together with the three slots: the protected unit, the accounting scope, and the release boundary.</p></details> |  |
| **[Beyond Retrieval Failure: Understanding Why LLMs Miss Evidence in Multi-Hop RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21184v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-hop retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) requires an agent to retrieve evidence for a sequence of dependent sub-questions. When a wrong answer lacks a required passage, the passage may be inaccessible to the retriever, or the agent may have failed to issue a query that would retrieve it. Standard retrieval evaluations conflate these two causes. We study how much apparent retrieval failure is instead caused by search drift: the search no longer tracks an unanswered sub-question. Holding the retriever, corpus, and generation model fixed, we introduce ExecutionRAG, a training-free requirement register that records open sub-questions and their supporting evidence. The register changes search guidance but not evidence access, so each gold passage it recovers certifies that the passage was reachable by the original retriever. On 1,000 MuSiQue questions, the number of wrong answers with unretrieved required evidence falls from 382 to 234 with all-MiniLM-L6-v2 and from 383 to 274 with Qwen3-Embedding-0.6B. These counts correspond to 69% to 44% and 66% to 48% of each system's wrong answers, respectively. Crucially, evidence recovery does not produce a proportional reduction in total error: premature resolutions and abstentions increase as missing-evidence failures decrease. The central finding is therefore a shift in the remaining bottleneck, from guiding search toward required evidence to verifying that the retrieved evidence is sufficient.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by DAI 2026 Oral</p></details> |
| **[When RAG Doesn't Help: LLMs for Plausible Material Prediction of Vehicle Components](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18437v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In this work, we explore whether LLMs can predict and explain plausible materials for vehicle components such as brake discs or fuel injectors without extensive fine-tuning. We compare a standard generative LLM baseline, single-pass Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG), and an iterative Chain-of-Verification (CoVe) variant across two LLMs, using a domain-filtered Wikipedia corpus that proved helpful in prior work. As no gold standard exists, we develop and release a reusable web-based annotation tool supporting crucial functions for structured domain-expert judgments, such as highlighting, ratings and ranking. Expert agreement is reasonable for plausibility, lower for ratings. LLM-based generation compares favorably to prior work. RAG and CoVe do not improve on it and are partly rated worse by experts. Contrary to prior work, Wikipedia-based retrieval does not help in our setup.</p></details> |  |
| **[When More Documents Hurt RAG: Measuring and Reducing Vector Search Dilution with Domain-Scoped Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.11350v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) degrades when applied to large, heterogeneous document collections. In RAG systems, dense similarity becomes less discriminative, causing top-$k$ retrieval to return semantically similar but contextually incorrect chunks. We refer to this failure mode as vector search dilution. Even under hybrid dense+sparse retrieval, we first observed it in a deployed Wyoming Department of Transportation (WYDOT) system; in a controlled experiment, growing the corpus from 54 to 1,128 documents cut precision@10 by 63% and recall of the answer-bearing document by 23%. Similar patterns were observed in six other DOTs. To diagnose this issue, we introduce MASDR-RAG (Metadata-Scoped Domain Retrieval for RAG) as a controlled testbed over 200 human-validated queries, five LLM backbones, six public benchmark corpora, and two index stacks. In MASDR-RAG, domain scoping is the load-bearing component. We found hard partitioning to be unsafe, whereas training-free soft scoping is safe when the scope structure is real. Furthermore, we can discover these scopes without hand-curated metadata. However, multi-agent orchestration triggers a precision-faithfulness paradox that survives format controls, prompt controls, and blind human evaluation. Based on these varied outcomes, our practical recommendation is simple: soft-scope first, then perform a single synthesis call.</p></details> | <details><summary>19 pa...</summary><p>19 pages, 5 figures, 20 tables. Preprint under review at ACL ARR October</p></details> |
| **[Configuring RAG Systems: What Breaks, Where Originates, and How to Fix](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18567v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) has emerged as a dominant paradigm for improving Large Language Model (LLM) by intertwining it with other components, such as a vector/graph database. Therefore, the resulting RAG systems are often highly configurable, under which a non-trivial amount of failures are related to the configurations. However, there remains a lack of systematic understanding regarding the configuration issues for RAG systems. To bridge this gap, in this paper, we conduct the first large-scale empirical study of 654 real-world configuration issues collected from 10 diverse RAG systems. Drawing on this, we provide a complete categorization of the symptoms, root causes, relevant stages, and fix patterns of configuration issues for RAG systems, as well as several new observations. Our findings not only reveal the characteristics of configuration issues, but also strongly link them to the unique pipeline stages of RAG, e.g., we found that most symptoms can be observed at the early or late stage, while the stage of the root cause might not be at the same stage as where the symptom is identified. We also provide nine actionable insights to the practitioners on testing, localizing, and fixing the configuration issues for RAG systems.</p></details> | 28 pages, 7 figures |
| **[Copying Is Not Grounding: Training Faithful Long-Form RAG with Authored-Sentence Credit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18442v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In search engines and assistants, long-form RAG answers can mislead readers with claims their sources do not support. Faithfulness rewards, the share of supported sentences, train models toward fewer such claims. We show that these rewards are hacked by copying. A sentence copied from the passages is supported almost by construction, so trained models learn to copy rather than ground what they write. Common defenses only move the hack: a KL or copy penalty leaves copying in place or trades it for shorter, emptier answers and refusals. GRPO, the standard RL algorithm, makes it worse by giving every sentence its answer's advantage, reinforcing copied and grounded sentences alike. We propose Authored-Sentence Credit (ASC) , which fixes both by moving GRPO's reward and advantage from the answer to the sentence level. The reward credits only sentences that are authored rather than copied, say more than the question, and are supported by the passages. The advantage compares each sentence with every sentence sampled for the question, so one that earns no credit ranks last and is never reinforced. Across four models and two in-domain, three held-out corpora, only ASC lowers the error rate of authored sentences and writes more supported ones in every setting, by 38% and 46% at the median under held-out evaluators, giving readers more grounded information. It takes none of these shortcuts, and two LLM judges comparing whole answers prefer no baseline to the base model but rate ASC's answers on par with it. We conclude that a faithfulness reward should credit what a model writes, not what it copies.</p></details> |  |
| **[PerCache: Predictive Hierarchical Cache for RAG Applications on Mobile Devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.11553v2)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) has become a de facto paradigm in various large language model (LLM)-driven applications on mobile devices, such as mobile assistants leveraging personal emails or meeting records. However, due to lengthy prompts and resource constraints, mobile RAG systems exhibit significantly high response latency. To address this issue, one promising approach is to reuse intermediate computational results across different queries to eliminate redundant computation. But most existing approaches, such as key-value (KV) cache reuse and semantic cache reuse, are designed for cloud settings and perform poorly on mobile devices as they overlook the distinctive characteristics of mobile RAG. We propose PerCache, a novel hierarchical cache framework designed for reducing end-to-end latency of personalized RAG applications on mobile platforms. PerCache adopts a hierarchical architecture that progressively matches similar queries and query-key-value (QKV) cache to maximize the reuse of intermediate results at different computing stages. To improve cache hit rate, PerCache applies a predictive method to populate the cache with queries that are likely to be raised in the future. In addition, PerCache can adapt its configurations to dynamic system loads, aiming to maximize caching utility with constrained resource consumption. We implement PerCache on top of an existing mobile LLM inference engine and deploy it on commodity mobile phones. Extensive evaluations show that PerCache achieves the lowest average end-to-end latency across five datasets and two LLM architectures, reducing latency by up to 63.5% over the strongest baseline while maintaining comparable answer quality.</p></details> |  |
| **[CacheSmith: Accurate RAG Serving under Latency SLOs with Adaptive KV Reuse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18925v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This paper presents CacheSmith, a KV cache reuse system for RAG prefill that maximizes quality-gated SLO attainment. While context caching lowers prefill latency by reusing precomputed KV states for retrieved chunks, it misses cross-chunk attention that a full prefill would compute over concatenated inputs. Prior selective recomputation schemes tackle this problem but enforce static, one-size-fits-all configurations that fail to effectively handle request heterogeneity or dynamic latency budgets. CacheSmith decomposes selective recomputation into a three-phase workflow of WarmUp, Probe, and Patch that can be dynamically configured to exploit a wide range of cost-accuracy trade-offs. At serving time, an SLO-aware scheduler in CacheSmith forms batches to mitigate inter-request interference during layer-by-layer prefill, while a budget-aware planner configures recomputation phases across the batch to maximize the number of requests satisfying both generation quality and latency targets. Both components make fast, solver-free decisions over profiled cost-accuracy bands. On four benchmarks across varying arrival rates and burstiness levels, CacheSmith improves quality-gated SLO attainment by up to 27.5%, over four state-of-the-art baselines.</p></details> |  |
| **[EvoRAG: Making Knowledge Graph-based RAG Automatically Evolve through Feedback Backpropagation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.15676v2)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Knowledge Graph-based Retrieval-Augmented Generation (KG-RAG) has emerged as a promising paradigm for enhancing LLM reasoning by retrieving multi-hop paths from KGs. However, existing KG-RAG frameworks often underperform in real-world scenarios because the pre-captured knowledge dependencies are not tailored to the downstream task or its evolving requirements. These frameworks struggle to adapt to task-specific requirements and lack mechanisms to filter low-contribution knowledge during generation. We observe that feedback on generated responses offers effective supervision for improving KG quality, as it directly reflects user expectations and provides insights into the correctness and usefulness of the output. However, a key challenge lies in effectively linking response-level feedback to triplet-level contribution evaluation and knowledge updates in the KG. In this work, we propose EvoRAG, a self-evolving KG-RAG framework that leverages the feedback over generated responses to continuously refine the KG and enhance reasoning accuracy. EvoRAG introduces a feedback-driven backpropagation mechanism that attributes feedback to retrieved paths by measuring their utility for response and propagates this utility back to individual triplets, supporting fine-grained KG refinements towards more adaptive and accurate reasoning. Through EvoRAG, we establish a closed loop that couples feedback, LLM, and graph data, continuously enhancing the performance and robustness in real-world scenarios. Experimental results show that EvoRAG improves reasoning accuracy by $7.34\%$ over state-of-the-art KG-RAG frameworks. The source code has been made available at https://github.com/iDC-NEU/EvoRAG.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by ICDE 2027</p></details> |
| **[Fast and Accurate: Improving Personalized LLM Performance by Semantic Concentration Inspired Workload Split between RAG and Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15921v1)** | 2026-10-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>With ever increasing personalized large language model (LLM) services, the next question to answer is how to improve the quality of services/users' experiences, which is indicated by personalized LLM generation accuracy/quality and inference latency. Although state-of-the-art designs, e.g., parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT), retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) or Full PEFT+RAG, can help, they largely ignore the heterogeneous semantic concentrations of users' historical data and cannot fully unleash the potential of personal data for personalized LLM performance enhancement. In this paper, we propose PEFT Union RAG (PUR), a novel ``fast and accurate'' approach that exploits the semantic concentration of users' personal historical data to split workload between PEFT and RAG, aiming to improve personalized LLM performance in terms of better generation accuracy/quality and lower inference latency. Briefly, PUR partitions user's personal historical data and assigns semantic concentrated ``core'' information to PEFT for offline parametric fine-tuning, while retaining dispersed information for RAG. During the online LLM inference, PUR directly generates responses for ``core'' queries covered by the PEFT region and invokes RAG only for sporadic ``peripheral'' queries. Experimental results show that, by appropriately splitting workload between PEFT and RAG, PUR consistently achieves the best generation accuracy/quality for various users across multiple tasks, while reducing inference latency by 14.4%--46.6% compared with the best performed method.</p></details> |  |
| **[OntoAR-HAR: Ontology-Guided Agentic RAG with Personalized Memory for Human Activity Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15292v1)** | 2026-10-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLMs increasingly support sensor-based human activity recognition (HAR) by linking motion signals with activity semantics and physical knowledge. Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) grounds decisions in sensor examples and domain features, enabling training-free target-user inference with less dependence on raw numerical arrays. However, existing LLM/RAG-based HAR typically searches single-level activity spaces using static population knowledge. Consequently, they compare many unrelated candidates using the same static population evidence and overlook personalized motion patterns. We propose OntoAR-HAR, an ontology-guided agentic RAG framework combining static population and personalized knowledge. Its tree-structured activity ontology decomposes classification into local decisions among semantically related activities. The static store supplies node-constrained examples, while offline-discovered executable signal features provide numerical evidence for local distinctions. During inference, the personalized store collects selected user observations, which the LLM integrates with static knowledge and signal features while traversing the ontology to an activity label. Cross-subject and heterogeneous-sensing experiments show complementary gains from hierarchical retrieval and personalized memory across multiple LLM backbones, achieving state-of-the-art performance. By explicitly modeling hierarchical relations across activity granularities, OntoAR-HAR also addresses the long-overlooked semantic inclusion problem and avoids treating related labels as independent categories.</p></details> |  |
| **[Retrieving Complementary Evidence for Multi-Hop RAG with Semantic Gain Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15285v1)** | 2026-10-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-hop retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) requires finding documents whose evidence is useful in combination. Query relevance and graph connectivity help locate related content, but neither directly measures the additional information one document contributes given another. We introduce SG$^2$RAG, a framework that represents this complementarity through semantic gain. Offline, a large language model assesses the relevant and nonredundant information contributed by one chunk given another within neighborhoods proposed by semantic similarity. These ordered assessments are aggregated into symmetric edge weights to construct a reusable Semantic Gain Graph. Online, controlled Personalized PageRank starts from query-relevant seeds and ranks complementary evidence beyond the initial retrieval set. | **[How To Train Your World Model: Fine-tuning vs RAG for LM-based World Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02542v1)** | 2026-10-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>World models (WMs) simulate the transition dynamics of environments, enabling agents to plan over the consequences of their actions. | **[Coordination Matters: Process-Aware Evaluation of Cooperative Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06557v2)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) benchmarks commonly emphasize aggregate outcomes such as return, success rate, or completion time. While essential, these metrics can obscure how agents coordinate and fail to distinguish policies that achieve similar outcomes through different interaction processes. We propose a process-aware evaluation framework that complements aggregate performance with diagnostics of interference, coordinated task completion, participation, and coordination efficiency. We evaluate the framework in STAT, a controlled spatial task-allocation testbed, and examine its broader applicability in two established cooperative MARL benchmarks. Across diverse value-based and actor-critic methods, we find policies with nearly identical return can exhibit substantial differences in coordination behavior. Controlled scaling in STAT reveals distinct process signatures for spatial, task, and agent scaling, while a reward-shaping ablation shows that explicitly penalizing assignment conflicts can alter coordination behavior with little change in task return. Results in the established benchmarks further show that the framework remains informative under different coordination mechanisms. These findings support process-aware evaluation as a complement to outcome-based benchmarking. The framework provides a common structure for selecting and interpreting environment-specific diagnostics that characterize not only what cooperative policies achieve, but how they coordinate to achieve it.</p></details> | Under review |
| **[CocoRNA: Collective RNA Design with Cooperative Multi-agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23820v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Designing RNA sequences that reliably fold into specific secondary structures is essential for understanding their biological functions but remains a challenging computational problem. This approach reduces the complexity of the problem and improves the exploration of design policies. During training, a centralized critic uses global structural information to guide the agents, enabling them to jointly optimize their design strategies. As a result, CocoRNA learns high-quality RNA design policies that generalize effectively to unseen structures without additional training. Experiments on the Rfam dataset demonstrate that CocoRNA substantially outperforms state-of-the-art methods in both success rate and design speed. Further experiments on other biological sequence design tasks highlight the effectiveness and broad potential of CocoRNA for complex design tasks.</p></details> | ICML 2026 |
| **[Jarvis: Coordination Deserves a Brain of Its Own in Agentic Interactive Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23804v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Interactive models increasingly hand complex reasoning and tool use to a slow brain in the background, leaving a realtime front model free to sustain full-duplex dialogue. Yet the user may refine, cancel or replace a request while its task runs, so a plan or result that made sense a moment ago goes stale. Each new utterance must therefore be read against the work in flight, but the front model follows only the conversation and the back end sees only its own task. Coordination deserves a brain of its own, one that sees both. We introduce Jarvis, a coordination harness where models judge and code enforces. Its typed decision model, JEV, reads each turn against the live tasks and picks among operations built from a versioned task record. Code checks every choice against the current state before applying it, so no decision can act on a task version the user has replaced. Jarvis also rechecks plans before they execute and results before they are spoken, and counts an answer as delivered only once the user has heard it in full. We build two instances of this design. Jarvis-Audio runs unchanged on three full-duplex speech models. Jarvis-Omni adds vision and rechecks the evidence behind an answer before and during playback, since a scene can change while nobody speaks. With the front model and back end held fixed, replacing Qwen Audio Agent with Jarvis raises tau-Voice task success more than sixfold, from 29 to 192 of 278 tasks, and the gains hold on every benchmark and front model. Against published systems, Jarvis-Audio reaches 69% Pass1 on Full-Duplex-Bench v3 with an open 9B front model, versus 60% for GPT-Realtime. These results suggest that how the two brains are coordinated matters as much as how strong they are.</p></details> |  |

Admitted skills guide subsequent tasks and are revised through feedback. Evaluation on MolBench and SMDD-Bench demonstrates capabilities in molecular screening, optimization and constrained design. | **[Where Do Multi-Agent Systems Fail? Evidence-Grounded Diagnosis of Collective Mechanisms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38761v2)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>When a multi-agent system answers correctly, it is tempting to conclude that its agents shared, checked, and used information as intended. We ask what evidence from an execution is sufficient to conclude that a particular mechanism was violated. Our answer is a diagnostic contract, which separates what counts as a violation from which execution records can establish one, and concludes that a violation is supported, ruled out, or unknown; removing records can make this conclusion unknown but never reverse it. We test contracts for four mechanisms by replaying executions from the step where a mechanism acts, once unchanged, once with the mechanism broken, and once with it restored. The contracts also applied, in narrow form, to mechanisms in independently developed systems, but a diagnostic behavior that was nearly perfect on our benchmark degraded on an independently developed workflow. An internal large language model (LLM) agent reads the task and evidence, selects a permitted tool action or request for human feedback, and uses checked results to decide whether to continue, revisit an analysis, switch a candidate, or stop. The harness supplies tool integration, controlled execution, state management, historical validation, and execution limits. npm distribution connects installation and configuration to interactive execution and forecast and report export. Across five public electricity-price datasets, CastClaw achieves the lowest point-estimate MSE and MAE among 16 baselines. A Nord Pool case study illustrates its inspectable decision process, while provincial electricity-load data from North China (January--June 2026) demonstrate offline task execution.</p></details> |  |
| **[Understanding KV Residency in Production-Scale Agentic LLM Workloads](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22863v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic large language model (LLM) applications turn one user request into a sequence of model and tool calls over a growing context. Retaining this context's key-value (KV) state in GPU memory lets later calls reuse it instead of recomputing it, but at fleet scale this needs substantial memory. We characterize four production Microsoft M365 workload traces (Deep Research, Enterprise Assistant, Coding Agent and Enterprise Chat) spanning more than 121 million model calls. In Deep Research, the application issues 89% of model calls and the median context grows from about 10K to 68K tokens by the 30th call. About 80% of context tokens are served from the prefix cache. With the public Llama-2-70B KV layout standing in for confidential models, one 99th-percentile Deep Research session exceeds the 80 GB HBM of an NVIDIA H100, and holding all live sessions at their peak needs an estimated 501.8 TB of HBM, although inference occupies only 5.6% of that retention time. Trace-driven replay shows that a one-hour idle cap cuts this ceiling by 3.6x while raising the share of calls that recompute context only from 4.07% to 4.53%. Under tighter budgets, eviction alone is insufficient: at one tenth of the busiest hour's demand, LRU eviction makes 63% of Deep Research calls recompute and clairvoyant furthest-next-use 31%, whereas multi-resolution retention (MRR), which demotes the LRU session's KV state to int4, lowers this to 13.6%. Placement imposes a separate limit: when the next call reaches a different deployment, cached-context reuse drops from 89% to 29%, and treating KV state as deployment-local raises the recompute floor under unlimited memory from 5.4% to 20.2%. These findings motivate retention that changes the representation of KV state, together with deployment-aware routing or migration. We plan to release AgenticTraceSim, a parametric trace generator, and a residency replay framework.</p></details> |  |
| **[LoongSage: A Production-Grade, High-Efficiency Agentic RL Framework for Frontier LLMs with Validated Recipes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16352v2)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement learning (RL) has emerged as a critical phase in the post-training pipeline of large language models (LLMs). As the frontier of LLM capabilities shifts from static reasoning to agentic tasks--characterized by external tool invocation, multi-turn interactions, and long-horizon decision-making--existing mainstream RL frameworks have grown increasingly unwieldy, raising the overhead of adoption, usage, and debugging. To address these challenges, we present LoongSage, an agent-native, fully asynchronous RL framework with a lightweight core of roughly 14K lines of code. Built upon Megatron for distributed training and SGLang for high-throughput inference, LoongSage centralizes orchestration within a single controller process and encapsulates all GPU-resident components as Ray actors. It introduces AgentFlow, a unified execution paradigm that orchestrates both white-box and black-box agents. For asynchronous execution, it integrates sliding-window strategies that bound trajectory staleness, and employs TransferMesh, a topology-aware synchronization mechanism that routes weights after each step over the fastest transport available to each receiver. Beyond agentic RL, LoongSage incorporates a memory-efficient Teacher Manager that decouples the scaling of teacher models from GPU memory constraints, natively supporting Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation. Extensive evaluations across four complete training runs, spanning complex reinforcement learning and multi-teacher distillation, demonstrate that task rewards improve steadily while the train-inference gap is kept under continuous control, and the fully asynchronous pipeline removes synchronous idle bubbles, sustaining hardware utilization.</p></details> |  |

## SFT
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[Multi-SFT: Permissively-Licensed Multilingual Instruction-Following](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22086v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Supervised fine-tuning datasets remain overwhelmingly English-centric and commercially restricted, as their content is frequently generated by proprietary models whose terms of service forbid training competing systems. We introduce Multi-SFT, a commercially permissive collection of 502,818 instruction-response pairs across five European languages, built via translation and regeneration pipelines for tasks where translation is insufficient. Human evaluation yields inter-annotator agreement of at least 0.87 across all datasets and languages. Fine-tuning nine models from three different families (EuroLLM, OLMo 2, Qwen3; 1B-32B) and evaluating them on 13 benchmarks shows that Multi-SFT alone is competitive with larger augmented mixes and official instruction-tuned releases, with decisive advantages on safety tasks.</p></details> | 13 pages, 7 tables |
| **[Match the Canvas, Not Just the Answer: Predictability-Aware SFT for Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18001v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Diffusion language models (DLMs) enable flexible parallel generation by resolving tokens according to the model's evolving predictability rather than a fixed left-to-right order. This flexibility, however, introduces training choices absent from standard autoregressive teacher forcing: which reference tokens are visible when a target is supervised and how much generation space is allocated. Standard supervised fine-tuning (SFT) typically makes these choices through random masking, independently of inference. Our analysis shows how this training-inference mismatch can misdirect supervision. Random masking can conceal prediction errors or penalize uncertainty that decoding would otherwise resolve, while the attention-visible input length can change which response formulations the model favors, even when the underlying solution is unchanged. Motivated by these findings, we propose Predictability-Aware Canvas Selection and Supervision (PACS), an SFT method. PACS uses the model's own predictability to select each example's attention-visible input length and supervises reference tokens immediately before their confidence-guided revelation. Across reasoning and code-generation benchmarks, PACS consistently achieves the highest mean accuracy among the compared SFT methods, with gains of up to 12.84 percentage points over vanilla SFT on individual benchmarks. Code will be released upon publication.</p></details> |  |
| **[SFT Leaves a Reproducible Signature in Activation Geometry: Calibrating Post-Training Interpretability Against Run-to-Run Noise](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17093v1)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A model diff credits post-training with whatever separates a base checkpoint from one post-trained descendant, presuming a rerun would land close by. On the sparse co-activation graph of Qwen3-1.7B it does not: two LoRA-SFT reruns sit over half as far apart as either sits from the base. We score each base-to-post contrast against the run-to-run band of its own recipe, and show that $k$ runs certify no better than $p=1/(k+1)$ under run exchangeability, however large the effect, so the band $z$-score is a descriptive scale. SFT leaves a large, reproducible signature: base $\to$ SFT displaces the graph by 17.8 pairwise-distance SDs beyond the mean distance between four independent LoRA-SFT runs, and the pre-registered estimator replicates on Llama-3.1-8B with Llama-Scope. At 29 steps each, four SFT and four DPO runs occupy disjoint displacement ranges, a gap $5.0\times$ and $5.6\times$ the spreads of the two arms, without any band. All bands use graphs built on one GPU architecture. Three other readings stop short of a verdict: DPO band counts (3/4, 1/4, 3/4 contrasts outside at 12, 29, 60 steps) show no consistent dose ordering while its displacement stays flat; light GRPO is a secondary non-detection, not an absence, and we run no equivalence test; and the graph concentration read as an RL signature is already in the zero-RL base. A pre-registered causal sweep at three edge budgets on 4,816 held-out prompts found the displaced sources load-bearing ($12\times$ a matched random control in mean, $3.5\times$ in median at 10,000 edges), but their stage-specificity interaction reversed at two budgets and the third failed its faithfulness gate, so we claim calibrated structural displacement, not a changed mechanism. Resources: Qwen3.5-4B compact-backbone release: https://huggingface.co/YueLinHu/GAC-Qwen3.5-4B; reference implementation: https://github.com/deepnovacore/GAC; GAC project page: https://deepnovacore.github.io/GAC/</p></details> | <details><summary>17 pa...</summary><p>17 pages, 3 figures, 22 tables. | **[What Should SFT Learn? Conditional Information Gain for Generalizable Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12995v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) remains a widely used approach for post-training large language models, because fixed demonstrations can directly introduce desired reasoning strategies and behavioral patterns. Recent dynamic SFT methods mitigate this issue by reweighting token-level supervision according to model-side signals such as predictive probability, prior support, or distributional compatibility. We propose CIG-SFT, an information-theoretic framework that estimates the task-conditioned information carried by each demonstrated token. Under teacher forcing, we compare the token likelihood from a frozen reference model with and without access to the task input while keeping the gold solution prefix fixed. The resulting conditional information gain quantifies how much the task itself reduces uncertainty about the demonstrated decision. We then convert this information into a KL-regularized projection of the current policy and derive an equivalent scalar-weighted SFT objective whose logit gradient exactly matches that of the projected target. Across mathematical reasoning and code-generation benchmarks, CIG-SFT consistently outperforms standard SFT and recent adaptive fine-tuning baselines, with particularly strong improvements in generalization to challenging evaluation distributions, while requiring neither additional training data nor online rollouts.</p></details> |  |

We formulate data rewriting as a policy-learning problem and train a lightweight LoRA rewriting policy with reinforcement learning. The policy optimizes question-answering-style distributional alignment and semantic diversity under a hard task-consistency gate, producing verified supervision for downstream SFT. Across three instruction-tuned backbones, the resulting models attain downstream gains broadly comparable to standard SFT while reducing degradation on non-downstream benchmarks in every evaluated setting. | **[Unifying ICL, SFT, KL-Regularized RL Through a Bayesian Lens](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05111v2)** | 2026-09-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Supervised fine-tuning (SFT), few-shot in-context learning (ICL), KL-regularized RLHF/RLVR, and on-policy distillation are usually treated as distinct post-training paradigms. This yields a single chain of equivalences: few-shot ICL is an amortized projection onto the Bayes posterior predictive, and reward-weighted SFT, reward-weighted ICL, and advantage-weighted SFT are forward-KL projections of reward-induced Gibbs posteriors. A theoretical note</p></details> |

| **[Which Tokens Should SFT Actually Learn? We instantiate this principle with a Gaussian weight centered at margin m with bandwidth τ, requiring no reference model or additional forward pass. We evaluate TrimSFT on six base models from the Llama, Qwen, and DeepMath families across five mathematical reasoning benchmarks. TrimSFT consistently improves over standard SFT, achieving the best average performance on five out of six models, with gains of up to +26.9 points over SFT on MATH500. It both mitigates the reward sparsity problem of GRPO and avoids the instance order dependency problem of SFT. To evaluate particularly challenging cases, we curate ATS, a dataset for artistic text spotting. Conflict-SFT and DPO largely use the same causal attention-head set as the starting model. | **[Thinking in a Low-Resource Language: What SFT Builds, What RL Fixes, What Accuracy Cannot See](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17744v1)** | 2026-08-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Take three frontier mixture-of-experts models (Alibaba, OpenAI, NVIDIA; 3.6-4.0B active parameters each) and fine-tune them to reason in a low-resource language. That null is our first result. The real changes live where accuracy cannot see. What SFT cannot do is fix its own defects: a quarter of answers skip the requested format, answers leak into the reasoning channel, and an explicit "think in English" is obeyed under half the time. Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards, pre-registered before training, fixes the first two outright (fallback 24% to 2.5%, leak 3.5% to 0.0%, both against a flat random-reward control) and moves the third (+9.1pp), while the Greek reasoning habit survives an accuracy-only gradient untouched. | **[Fine-Tuning Qwen3-27B for C-to-Rust Code Translation: A Three-Stage Curriculum of Pretraining, Debugging-Aware SFT, and Task-Specific SFT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13681v1)** | 2026-08-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Translating C code into safe, idiomatic Rust is a longstanding software-engineering goal because it can eliminate entire classes of memory-safety vulnerabilities while preserving the functional behavior of legacy systems. ## RLHF
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[CleanRM: Reward Modeling from Noisy Feedback with a Few Clean Feedback for RLHF](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21138v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Feedback noise is pervasive in preference data, arising from subjective human judgments, annotation mistakes, and imperfect LLM judges. Standard reward modeling trained on such feedback yields a biased estimate of the clean risk. Although classical denoising methods are directly applicable to noisy feedback learning, they generally assume a uniform noise ratio across samples. However, this assumption fails for feedback data, where noise is intrinsically sample-wise. To address this challenge, we propose CleanRM, which uses a few clean feedback to calibrate sample-wise noise ratios via optimal transport. Specifically, CleanRM aligns the joint distribution of preferences reverted from noisy feedback with that of clean feedback and then trains the reward model using a noise-corrected surrogate loss. Theoretically, we prove that the resulting objective is unbiased with respect to the clean risk. Extensive experiments demonstrate that CleanRM outperforms state-of-the-art denoising baselines and yields more effective downstream RLHF alignment.</p></details> |  |
| **[Rater State Bias in RLHF Preference Data: An Audit Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16195v3)** | 2026-09-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We identify a structured confound in Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF). Pairwise preference labels are intended to reflect the compared outputs, but they may also reflect the rater's state during annotation. Under sustained stressful or distressing conditions, raters' preferences may shift over time, so that preference data encode rater state alongside judgments about response quality. We argue that, if present, such shifts would differ from ordinary disagreement or random label noise. They would be state dependent, could be shared across annotators under similar conditions, and would not necessarily cancel during aggregation, reward modeling, and policy optimization. We propose rater state shift as a plausible and testable source of structured bias in RLHF preference data. This paper develops a hypothesis and an audit framework for studying this source of bias. We define rater state shift, rater state confound, and correlated rater state bias. We also propose survival level emotional authenticity as a candidate output signature, defined by lexical, pragmatic, discourse, and safety features whose reliability and validity remain to be demonstrated. We show that systematic rater state bias can survive aggregation and may enter the learned reward signal. We state five testable predictions, together with effect size thresholds for an initial audit, and note which require proprietary data. Finally, we present an audit protocol and pilot study plan that can be applied to publicly available instruction tuned models. We do not infer the training history of any specific deployed model.</p></details> | <details><summary>v3: R...</summary><p>v3: Refined the mathematical analysis of bias propagation and policy optimization. Extended the discussion to model judges and the possible transmission of preference bias across models. Minor revisions throughout</p></details> |
| **[Reward Shaping to Mitigate Reward Hacking in RLHF](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.18770v8)** | 2026-09-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF) is widely used to align large language models (LLMs) with human preferences. However, RLHF remains vulnerable to \emph{reward hacking}, whereby a policy exploits imperfections in the reward function instead of learning the intended behavior, thereby undermining alignment. Although reward shaping can stabilize RLHF training and partially mitigate reward hacking, shaping methods and their underlying design principles have not been systematically investigated. To address this gap, we conduct a comprehensive study of prevalent reward-shaping techniques. Our analysis identifies two key design principles: (1) the reinforcement-learning reward should be bounded, and (2) it should grow rapidly at first and then gradually saturate. Motivated by these principles, we propose Preference as Reward (PAR), a novel method that uses the latent preferences encoded in the reward model as the reinforcement-learning signal. We further show that PAR possesses two variance-reduction properties that stabilize RLHF training and substantially widen the practical window for early stopping. Our evaluation consists of two parts. First, we compare PAR with several other reward-shaping strategies using Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) as the reinforcement-learning algorithm and Gemma2-2B as the base model. Second, we compare PAR with the vanilla baseline (i.e., unshaped reward) across four base models and four reinforcement-learning algorithms. In the first set of experiments, PAR consistently outperforms other reward-shaping methods and also reflects high data efficiency and robustness. The second set of experiments shows that PAR is particularly effective for actor-critic RL algorithms when value estimates become unstable and demonstrates its effectiveness across different base models. The code is available at https://github.com/PorUna-byte/PAR.</p></details> |  |
| **[Distortion of AI Alignment Revisited: RLHF is a Decent Utilitarian Aligner](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12651v1)** | 2026-09-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>While Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) is the standard paradigm for aligning large language models with human preferences, its effectiveness in pluralistic settings has been called into question. Notably, recent work by Gölz et al. (2025) demonstrated that the \textit{distortion} -- defined as the multiplicative gap between the average user utility of the RLHF policy and the optimal average utility -- can scale exponentially with the Bradley-Terry temperature parameter $β$ when users have heterogeneous preferences. In this work, we present a fine-grained analysis of the distortion of RLHF with reward clipping and demonstrate that such exponential degradation is not a fundamental property of the algorithm but rather a consequence of distribution mismatch between the distribution generating preference data ($μ$) and the KL reference policy ($π_{\mathrm{ref}}$). To this end, we establish tight upper and lower bounds on the distortion of RLHF across multiple regimes of the KL regularization strength. We show that in a representative regime, under the Bradley-Terry model, the distortion is $\tildeΘ(βB + β)$, where $B$ is an upper bound on the log density ratio between $μ$ and $π_{\mathrm{ref}}$. In particular, when there is no distribution mismatch (i.e., $μ= π_{\mathrm{ref}}$), RLHF achieves the optimal distortion of $O(β)$ up to a constant. Our results suggest that, to reasonably maximize average utility with RLHF, it is preferable to use on-policy sampled preference data or to fine-tune before RLHF on data from a source close to $μ$.</p></details> |  |

We further consider general pairwise preferences that need not admit a scalar reward representation and may exhibit cycles. In this setting, we construct a pessimistic von Neumann winner policy and derive corresponding performance guarantees. Under these models, our results provide a unified finite-sample solution to a central challenge in pluralistic alignment: learning from limited, heterogeneous, and potentially cyclic feedback, and producing a single policy with explicit collective-welfare guarantees. Building on this, we present Iterative GRPO, which alternates between fitting $Q^π$ from logged Monte Carlo returns via standard reward modeling (policy evaluation) and running single-turn GRPO against $Q^π$ (policy improvement). And yet most setups just default to PyTorch eager mode or torch.compile, no one checks if that's actually fastest. Scoring itself is small. Rollout generation eats far more of a typical RLHF step. But scoring and generation fight over the same CPU and GPU resources, so a faster scoring engine doesn't shrink step time on its own. It mainly frees up capacity generation can use instead. We built a native C++ inference engine on ONNX Runtime. First step: confirm correctness. Output matched the PyTorch reference to 5.7 x 10^-6 on CPU and 4.2 x 10^-3 on GPU, close enough to trust. Then we tested it against PyTorch eager mode, torch.compile, and FastAPI, on both CPU and GPU. CPU was decisive. Our engine beat every baseline, confidence intervals didn't even overlap. GPU gave a different view: we beat PyTorch and FastAPI, but torch.compile came out ahead. Further testing traced the speedup to ONNX Runtime itself, not C++ as a language. And batching strategy mattered more than either the language or the runtime choice, more than we expected. The results are from repeated, independent runs, since single runs just aren't reliable enough to trust.</p></details> |  |
